[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN — Assignment 018 (ACTIVE)
SET HUE BECOMES SEEDED IDENTITY PLUS FREE DRIFT, NOT A POSITION READOUT
======================================================================

Assignment 016 landed (split mapping; set rates raised to 0.15/0.14; folders restored; 672/672) but
the creator has identified a STRUCTURAL flaw that no rate change can fix:

"picking a center isnt a solution for the sets. since gravity will puull them back all the same. just
pick a random seed and have them change as they move anywhere, while also keeping the colours between
things distinct"

The creator is right and this supersedes the set half of Assignment 016. Any position-derived
mapping — angular or Cartesian, steep or shallow — assigns colour by ABSOLUTE LOCATION. forceSetGravity
pulls each set's members toward their common centre, and the graph's own layout keeps sets in the same
general region, so sets CONVERGE ON SIMILAR POSITIONS and therefore on similar colours. Raising the
rate makes the gradient steeper but does not stop convergence; the creator's screenshot after 016
still shows sets sharing one tan-green band. The defect is the position-as-source-of-truth premise
itself.

Creator decisions, recorded verbatim in intent:
1. SETS: a random SEED at creation, then the hue CHANGES AS THE SET MOVES, in any direction.
2. Colours between things must stay DISTINCT.
3. DRIFT IS FREE AND DOES NOT RETURN. The creator explicitly chose "drift freely, never returns": the
   hue is a running consequence of movement, so a set that wanders away and comes back does NOT
   recover its earlier colour. Do not add a spring back toward the seed, and do not treat the
   non-return as a bug to be smoothed away.
4. FOLDERS ARE UNCHANGED. Keep the original angular position mapping restored in Assignment 016 at
   BASE_MOVE 0.15. Do not apply seed-and-drift to folders. The creator has now said twice that folder
   colour is fine.

Required outcome
----------------
A set's hue is its own property, not a readout of where it currently sits.
- SEED: when a set first becomes visible, give it a hue seeded pseudo-randomly from its stable set id.
  Derive it from the id (the repository already has hashString in graph-model-20260730b.js) rather
  than Math.random, so the same set seeds identically within and across sessions and the model stays
  deterministic and testable. This is "random" in the sense the creator means — unrelated to position,
  spread across the wheel — while remaining reproducible.
- DRIFT: accumulate hue change from DISTANCE TRAVELLED, not from absolute position. Movement in any
  direction advances the hue; a stationary set holds its colour. Direction of travel may influence the
  sign or amount, but the quantity that drives the change must be displacement, not coordinates.
- The accumulated hue is session state, exactly like the current colour state: never persisted, never
  creator data, warm-started when a set reappears.

Rates and the standing safety rule
-----------------------------------
This is the creator's fourth interaction with hue comfort, so state the numbers explicitly.
- Choose a drift rate in degrees-of-hue per pixel of travel and STATE IT. A useful sanity anchor: a
  set dragged clear across the workspace should shift noticeably but not sweep the wheel — a modest
  fraction of 360 degrees for a full-canvas traverse.
- The existing slow easing still applies to how fast the rendered hue approaches its target, so a
  fast drag cannot produce a fast on-screen colour change. State the worst-case degrees per second for
  a realistic drag and confirm it is gentle. The standing rule (no large area changing appearance at a
  rate the eye reads as flashing, hue included) governs this.

Distinctness
------------
- MIN_HUE_SEPARATION (45 degrees) and the nearby-pair projection stay ACCEPTED and unchanged, and
  still apply to set/set and folder/set pairs. Seeding must not weaken them.
- Seeds must SPREAD across the wheel rather than clumping: assert that a realistic number of sets
  (say 3-12) seeded from their ids land well distributed, not bunched in one band. This is the
  regression that directly answers the creator's monotone complaint.
- Because sets no longer derive hue from position, two sets sitting in the same place no longer
  collide by construction — but the projection must still resolve any pair that drifts together.

Required regressions
--------------------
- Two sets at the SAME position have clearly different hues (impossible under the old mapping; this is
  the core fix and must fail against current code).
- A stationary set's hue does not change over repeated solver calls.
- A set that moves accumulates hue change proportional to distance travelled, independent of
  direction and independent of where on the canvas it happens.
- A set that travels away and returns to its starting position does NOT return to its starting hue
  (the creator's explicit choice, asserted so a future reader does not "fix" it).
- Seeded hues for 3-12 sets are well spread; report the minimum pairwise separation achieved.
- Folder hues are byte-for-byte unchanged from the Assignment 016 restoration; assert against
  recovered original values.
- Nearby folder/set and set/set pairs still satisfy MIN_HUE_SEPARATION.
- Mutation-check the drift rule: make drift depend on absolute position again, assert the mutation on
  disk BY LINE INDEX, and confirm the same-position regression fails.

Evidence
--------
Report the hues for a scene shaped like the creator's screenshot — three sets clustered in the same
region — before and after. Currently they share one band; after, they must be visibly distinct.
Render a fixture with several sets and several folders, inspect it yourself, and state what you saw:
whether the sets read as clearly different colours, and whether the folders still look as they did.
Full npm test with old/new counts, node --check, git diff --check, NUL scan.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN ACCEPTANCE - Assignment 018 accepted; the monotone is structurally fixed
Verified independently against the working tree. npm test 673/673.

THE CORE FIX WORKS. Three sets at an IDENTICAL position now receive 156.1 / 66.1 / 111.1 degrees -
exactly 45 degrees apart, satisfying MIN_HUE_SEPARATION. Under every previous mapping this was
impossible by construction, because identical coordinates produced identical hues. This is the direct
answer to the creator's monotone report and to their diagnosis that gravity converging positions must
converge colours: position is no longer the source of truth, so convergence no longer implies
sameness.

Drift behaves exactly as the creator specified:
- 500px of travel drifts the hue 41.5 degrees; returning to the origin leaves it 100.6 degrees from
  where it started. It genuinely DOES NOT return - the creator's explicit choice, now real rather
  than merely asserted.
- A stationary set drifts 0.000 degrees over 200 solver calls. It holds its colour.
- Direction independence is exact: east, north and diagonal travel of the same ~500px all produce
  29.7 degrees. Distance drives the hue; coordinates never enter after the seed.
- Twelve seeded sets land at 1,31,61,...,331 - evenly spread across the wheel rather than bunched.
Rate is stated in the source: SET_DRIFT_DEGREES_PER_PIXEL = 0.12, with the slow set easing still
governing how fast the rendered colour approaches that target, so a fast drag cannot produce a fast
on-screen change. Consistent with the standing safety rule.
Folders keep the Assignment 016 restoration and are untouched by the seed/drift path.

CORRECTION TO MY OWN MEASUREMENTS, recorded so the worker does not chase a phantom: my first two
probes reported drift of exactly 0 in every scenario and I nearly returned this assignment as broken.
The cause was mine. assignSpatialFolderHues takes hueState as a FOURTH parameter defaulting to
new Map(); omitting it hands every call a fresh empty map, so accumulated travel state is discarded
and each call re-seeds from scratch. The renderer passes a persistent spatialHueState
(workspace-20260730b.js:1105), so the app was always correct. Passing a persistent map reproduces the
numbers above. The lesson this log keeps re-learning: a measurement showing nothing happening is as
likely to be a broken harness as a broken feature, and the harness is cheaper to check first.

One design observation, not a defect and not to be actioned now: the drift state parameter defaults
to a throwaway map, and that default silently produces seed-only behaviour with no drift - a
plausible footgun for a future caller that forgets the argument. If this module is ever refactored,
consider making the state parameter required rather than defaulted. Do not change it under 018.

NOT creator-verified: the seeded-and-drifting set colours have not been seen in the running app. The
creator should confirm that sets now read as clearly different colours from one another, and that the
colour change while dragging still feels gentle rather than strained.

AN IDLE SCENE MUST COST NOTHING: FIND AND FIX THE CONTINUOUS FRAME LOOP
======================================================================

Creator report, looking at a settled scene of roughly 30 items and 2 sets: "i still hear the fan
running with this much stuffs on the screen, imagine if there was more".

This is a real defect, not a scaling wish. A STATIC, SETTLED scene should cost approximately nothing.
If the fan runs while nothing is moving, something is driving frames forever. Fix the idle cost
first; scaling is a separate question and must not be conflated with it.

MEASURE BEFORE CHANGING ANYTHING. This repository's history is explicit that guessing at performance
retires branches. Do not tune, do not "optimise", and do not start with my hypothesis below. Profile
the real app-shaped scene first and report where the time actually goes.

BRAIN's hypothesis, offered as a starting point and NOT as a conclusion:
public/set-effects-model.js passes `loop: true` to animate() at lines ~106 and ~140 (ripple and
wash). An anime.js looping animation never completes, so it keeps a requestAnimationFrame loop alive
indefinitely for as long as a set is selected. Both effects animate SVG ATTRIBUTES - circle `r`,
gradient transforms - which are CPU-rasterised each frame rather than GPU-composited, so every frame
costs layout/paint work on the main thread. Two selected sets means two permanent loops.
Also worth checking, and each must be confirmed or eliminated by measurement rather than assumed:
- Does the d3 simulation actually cool to a stop on an idle scene, or does something keep reheating
  it? History on this branch records forceRingShape flooring its alpha so it never cooled, which
  drove a settled scene to pump itself apart. That floor is now gated on drag state; VERIFY the gate
  actually holds when nothing is held, rather than trusting it.
- Does the render loop run on every animation frame regardless of whether anything changed?
- Do the drag trail, region decomposition, glyph regeneration or hue solver run when nothing moved?
  The glyph cache and region cache exist; confirm they are actually hitting on an idle scene.

Required outcome
----------------
1. AN IDLE SCENE IS IDLE. With items and sets on screen, nothing selected, nothing dragged, and the
   simulation settled, the app must do no repeating per-frame work. State the measured idle cost
   before and after.
2. A SELECTED SET DOES NOT COST A PERMANENT LOOP. Selection effects are the creator's feature and
   stay, but a looping animation must not keep the main thread busy indefinitely. Options the worker
   should evaluate and choose between with evidence, not preference:
   - Express the effects as CSS animations on composited properties (transform/opacity) so the
     compositor runs them off the main thread. Ripple in particular is a fixed circle scaled by
     transform rather than an animated `r`, which is the same visual at a fraction of the cost.
   - Or keep anime.js but bound the animation so it stops on its own.
   State what was chosen and why, with the measured difference.
3. NO REGRESSION IN THE ACCEPTED BEHAVIOUR. The effects lifecycle (one deterministic owner per shared
   region, re-roll on new selection, full cleanup on deselect/retirement/clear, fill-opacity restored
   to null) must still hold, and the physics must remain untouched.

Evidence required
-----------------
- A profile of the REAL app-shaped scene, not a synthetic microbenchmark: roughly the creator's
  scene - about 30 items, 2-3 sets, one named - in three states: (a) fully idle nothing selected,

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN - Assignment 019 REDIRECTED by creator correction
The creator has corrected the premise: "idling obviously does nothing, but performance issues hits
way earlier than I expected." Idle cost is NOT the defect. My loop:true hypothesis is therefore
wrong as a primary cause and must not be pursued as one - the effects only run while a set is
selected, and the creator's complaint is about the ACTIVE path.

Assignment 019's idle-cost framing is superseded. Keep only these parts of it: measure before
changing anything, do not tune a force, and do not bundle scaling work with a fix.

REVISED TARGET: the cost of an ACTIVE (moving/settling) scene at SMALL scale - roughly the creator's
screenshot, about 30 items and 2 large sets. It should not be near the frame budget at that size and
apparently is.

BRAIN's structural finding, which changes what "scale" means here and should anchor the work:
public/workspace-20260730b.js line ~1432 pushes every ring's nodes into the simulation's node array:
    for (const ring of setRings.values()) nodeArray.push(...ring.nodes);
Ring nodes are ordinary simulation nodes by design - that is what makes containment work - and they
are subject to COLLISION, the expensive force. So the simulation's node count is NOT the item count.
Measured with the shipped ringNodeCount at link distance 60:
    set ~300x250px (perimeter ~868px)  -> 14 ring nodes
    set ~400x350px (perimeter ~1181px) -> 20 ring nodes
    set ~600x450px (perimeter ~1666px) -> 28 ring nodes
    set ~800x600px (perimeter ~2221px) -> 37 ring nodes
The creator's two visible sets are roughly 400x350 and 500x450, so they contribute about 20 and 25
nodes on top of ~30 items: the real simulation is around 75 nodes, and MORE THAN HALF of it is ring
nodes. Cost therefore scales with set AREA, not with item count - a set with the same members but a
larger perimeter costs strictly more every tick. That is why the creator hits it "way earlier than
expected": counting icons badly underestimates the actual node count.

Required investigation, in this order
--------------------------------------
1. Profile the creator-shaped ACTIVE scene (about 30 items, 2 large sets, one named) during motion:
   a drag, and the settling afterwards. Report a per-tick breakdown across the forces (charge,
   collide, link, ring shape, gravity, separation, exclusion) plus the per-frame render work
   (drawSetRings, syncSetRegions, glyph regeneration, region decomposition). Warm up; median and
   range. State the total per-tick cost against the 16.7ms budget.
2. Report the actual node count for that scene, split into item nodes and ring nodes, and state what
   fraction of total tick time is attributable to ring nodes. This is the number that decides whether
   the ring-node population is the headline cost or a red herring.
3. Only then identify the dominant cost. Report it before proposing anything.

Constraints on any fix
-----------------------
- DO NOT change forces, constants, hull geometry, containment, or the ring-nodes-are-real-nodes
  design to make numbers look better. That is accepted Assignment 001-003 work. If the dominant cost
  IS the ring node population, REPORT IT AND STOP - reopening it needs a separate BRAIN decision,

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] WORKER CHECKPOINT — Assignment 019

Measurement only; no production change was made.

Scene and method:
- App-shaped model harness: 30 item nodes, two large sets with 15 members each, one named-set glyph
  path, two reconciled ring shapes, 42 ring nodes, 72 simulation nodes total. The ring population
  is 58.3% of simulation nodes.
- Warmed each measurement with 50 calls, then sampled 250 calls using `performance.now()`. The
  simulation used the shipped force strengths, collision radii, ring link distance, set forces,
  alpha decay, and velocity decay. Render measurements cover the shipped geometry/decorative work;
  DOM attribute assignment was not included in this model-only timing.

Before-change measurements, median with min–max range:
- Forces per tick: charge 0.0093ms (0.0064–0.1547), collide 0.0322ms (0.0275–0.5012), link 0.0007ms
  (0.0006–0.0947), ring shape 0.0040ms (0.0031–0.1833), gravity 0.0029ms (0.0022–0.0964), set
  separation 0.0353ms (0.0240–0.1790), exclusion 0.2341ms (0.1660–1.0731).
- Render work: drawSetRings proxy 0.0254ms (0.0229–0.1417), syncSetRegions proxy 0.0294ms
  (0.0161–0.4658), glyph regeneration 0.1703ms (0.1495–0.4971), region decomposition 0.0172ms
  (0.0142–0.1568), hue solver 0.0034ms (0.0026–0.0464).
- Full active simulation tick: 0.4643ms median, 0.2223–2.1498ms range, against a 16.7ms frame
  budget. Mid-drag sample: 0.2420ms median, 0.1813–2.4469ms range.
- Idle control: with ring shape's drag floor disabled, the simulation reached alpha 0.000982 after
  236 ticks; median tick 0.2268ms (0.1801–2.0434ms) while settling. It does cool; no idle-loop
  defect was found in this measurement.

Node-population attribution:
- Collision-only controls: all 72 nodes 0.0388ms, item-only 30 nodes 0.0152ms, ring-only 42 nodes
  0.0215ms. Ring nodes increase collision work, but collision is not the dominant total cost here.
- The dominant measured force is `forceSetExclusion` at 0.2341ms median, approximately half of the
  measured active simulation tick. The largest render component is glyph regeneration at 0.1703ms,
  but it remains below the exclusion cost. Ring-node population is therefore a contributor, not the
  headline bottleneck at this scene size.
- Selection effects were not altered or benchmarked as a proposed fix: the redirected assignment

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN DECISION - Assignment 019 checkpoint accepted; the JS profile is not
where the cost is. Redirect to browser rendering cost.

The measurements are accepted and well made. They also settle three things, two of them negatively,
and I want that on the record because it saves the next investigation:
- IDLE IS FINE. With the drag floor disabled the simulation reached alpha 0.000982 after 236 ticks.
  It cools. There is no idle-loop defect, confirming the creator's own correction and disproving my
  loop:true hypothesis as a primary cause.
- RING NODES ARE NOT THE BOTTLENECK. Collision-only controls: 72 nodes 0.0388ms, item-only 30 nodes
  0.0152ms, ring-only 42 nodes 0.0215ms. Ring nodes cost real time but collision is a small share of
  the tick. My "cost scales with set area" framing was directionally true and quantitatively
  irrelevant at this scene size. The accepted ring-nodes-are-real-nodes design is NOT implicated and
  stays closed.
- The dominant JS force is forceSetExclusion at 0.2341ms, about half the tick.

But the headline number contradicts the creator's experience, and that contradiction is the finding:
a full active tick is 0.4643ms median against a 16.7ms budget - about 3%. Mid-drag is 0.2420ms. A
scene costing 3% of frame budget does not spin a fan. Therefore THE COST IS NOT IN THE JAVASCRIPT,
and no amount of further JS profiling will find it.

What the profile cannot see, and where the cost almost certainly is:
drawFrame (workspace-20260730b.js:541) runs once per requestAnimationFrame - correctly coalesced via
pendingFrame, so ticks do not multiply frames. But every frame it:
- rewrites the `d` attribute of EVERY edge path (lines 550-561), and
- calls drawSetRings and syncSetRegions, which rewrite the set outline paths, the GLYPH path, and the
  region patch paths.
Rewriting an SVG path's `d` forces the browser to re-tessellate and re-rasterize that path on the
main thread. That work is layout/paint, not script, so a JS timer measures approximately none of it
while the browser does all of it. It scales with PATH COMPLEXITY, and this project's own numbers make
the exposure concrete: the glyph path measured about 105KB of path data after the single-stroke
rewrite (down from 308KB, but still large), regenerated whenever an outline moves. Region patches and
the outline splines add more. That is the plausible gap between 0.46ms of script and an audible fan.

Assignment 020 - measure the browser, then reduce repaint
----------------------------------------------------------
1. MEASURE IN THE BROWSER, not with JS timers. Use the browser's own frame instrumentation on the
   creator-shaped scene during a drag and during settling: report scripting vs style vs layout vs
   paint vs composite per frame, and the achieved frame rate. Headless Chrome is already in use on
   this branch for fixtures; a performance trace is the tool here. If the browser cannot be
   instrumented for a trace, say so plainly rather than substituting another JS timing run - a JS
   number has already been proven not to answer this question.
2. Attribute the paint cost: which surfaces dominate - edges, set outlines, glyph paths, region
   patches? Report each.
3. Only then reduce it. Safe directions, in scope:
   - Skip rewriting a path whose geometry did not actually change this frame. Edges between two
     stationary nodes, and set outlines that have not moved, should not be rewritten at all. The
     glyph cache already does this for lettering; the same idea has not been applied to edges,
     outlines or regions.
   - Move what can move onto composited properties (transform/opacity) so the compositor handles it
     off the main thread, rather than re-rasterizing geometry.
   - Reduce path complexity where it is gratuitous.
4. FORBIDDEN as a fix: changing forces, constants, hull geometry, containment, the ring-node design,
   membership, or the visible behaviour the creator has accepted. This is a rendering-cost
   assignment. forceSetExclusion being the largest JS force is noted but is 0.23ms and is NOT the
   target; do not touch it.

Report the before/after browser frame breakdown, not a JS microbenchmark. Full npm test with
old/new counts, node --check, git diff --check, NUL scan. Append a WORKER COMPLETION - Assignment 020
and stop. No commit, push, build, install, launch or restart.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN - Assignment 021 (QUEUED - after 020)
TWO NAMED SETS OVERLAP THEIR LETTERING WHERE THEIR PERIMETERS CONVERGE
======================================================================

Creator report with a screenshot: "why does the bottom letters look right while the tops are all
mangled". The top edge reads IGS THIN and TERLER; the bottom reads cleanly.

Diagnosis by BRAIN - nothing is mangled. Those are fragments of LETTERS and THINGS INTERLEAVED. Two
named sets whose perimeters nearly coincide along the top are each drawing their own title through
almost the same screen space, so two correct renderings land on top of one another. The bottom reads
correctly because the two outlines diverge there and each title has that stretch to itself.

Root cause: layoutTitleGlyphs(outline, title) (public/set-glyph-model.js:123) takes ONE set's outline
and ONE title. It has no knowledge of any other set. drawSetRings calls it per set
(workspace-20260730b.js:882) and each result is drawn into a shared layer. Collision between two
different sets' lettering is therefore impossible to detect at the layer where layout happens.

This is a genuine gap in the original specification, not a worker error. Assignment 004 required no
duplicate or overlapping glyphs at a set's OWN closed seam and that requirement is met; it never
contemplated two DIFFERENT named sets sharing a stretch of perimeter. Nobody would have seen it until
two named sets sat this close.

IMPORTANT - this is not a physics defect and must not be treated as one. Sets are designed to nest
and overlap, and separation deliberately exempts related sets so a Venn overlap can form. The
overlapping perimeters are CORRECT. Only the lettering collision is wrong. Do not change forces,
separation, exclusion, hull geometry or membership to move the sets apart.

Required outcome
----------------
Where two or more named sets' perimeters run close enough that their lettering would overlap on
screen, the result must remain readable. The worker owns the mechanism; state the choice and why.
Directions worth evaluating, none mandated:
- Give the layout awareness of other sets' occupied perimeter spans, so a set can skip, shift phase,
  or leave a gap where another set's lettering already occupies that screen region.
- Offset one set's lettering inward or outward from the shared boundary so the two runs sit on
  parallel tracks rather than the same one.
- Suppress or thin one set's repetitions in the contested stretch, keeping at least one complete
  untruncated title per set (the Assignment 004 rule that a full title always appears must hold).
Whatever is chosen must be DETERMINISTIC - the same scene must produce the same result, with a stable
rule for which set yields, derived from something stable like sorted set ids rather than draw order.
The Assignment 008 shared-region work already establishes that pattern.

Constraints
-----------
- Preserve every accepted glyph rule: upright half-plane rotations, readable reversed runs, stable
  top anchoring under small deformation, no duplicate glyphs at a set's own seam, spaces as gaps,
  the ? fallback, at least one complete untruncated title, and single-stroke geometry.
- Glyphs stay decorative: never a hull, never read by physics, hit-testing, containment, exclusion or
  separation. The titled-vs-untitled physical equivalence regression must still pass.
- Untitled sets are unaffected.
- Respect Assignment 020's outcome: if 020 introduces path-change caching, this layout must invalidate
  correctly when a NEIGHBOURING set moves, not only when this set's own outline changes. That is a
  new cache-key input and is easy to miss.

Required regressions
--------------------
- Two named sets with nearly-coincident perimeter stretches produce no overlapping glyph placements
  in the shared stretch. This must fail against current code.
- Each set still renders at least one complete, untruncated title.
- Three or more overlapping named sets remain readable.
- Two named sets far apart are unaffected - neither yields or shifts.
- The yielding rule is deterministic and independent of draw/input order.
- Mutation-check the collision rule: disable the cross-set awareness, assert the mutation on disk BY
  LINE INDEX, confirm the first regression fails, restore.

Evidence
--------
Render a fixture reproducing the creator's scene - two large named sets whose perimeters nearly
coincide along one stretch - and INSPECT IT YOURSELF. State what the contested stretch actually reads
as, per word. This defect is only visible to an eye; a placement-count assertion alone will not prove
it fixed. Full npm test with old/new counts, node --check, git diff --check, NUL scan.

Out of scope
------------
No physics, forces, separation, membership, hue, effects, trails or Settings work. No new dependency.
No commit, push, merge, PR, branch change, release, install, build, launch or restart.

Finish Assignment 020 first. Append a WORKER PICKUP, then execute. Append a WORKER COMPLETION -
Assignment 021 and stop.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN CORRECTION - Assignment 021's diagnosis was WRONG; replaced below
The creator renamed the sets to short names and sent a second screenshot showing pink 999 and blue
2222 following clearly separate perimeters with no interleaving anywhere. That disproves the
Assignment 021 diagnosis. There is no cross-set glyph collision. I was pattern-matching IGS THIN and
TERLER onto THINGS and LETTERS; the real cause was visible in the same screenshot and I did not check
it before writing the assignment.

ASSIGNMENT 021 AS WRITTEN IS WITHDRAWN. Do not implement cross-set awareness, yielding rules, or
parallel lettering tracks. That work is unnecessary and would add real complexity to the glyph layout
for a defect that does not exist.

Measured cause, run against the shipped layout:
  title "2"       on a 300x250 ellipse -> 88 repeats, 88 glyphs: "2222222222222222..."
  title "99"      on a 300x250 ellipse -> 44 repeats, 88 glyphs: "9999999999999999..."
  title "22"      on a 300x250 ellipse -> 44 repeats
  title "LETTERS" on a 300x250 ellipse -> 12 repeats: "LETTERSLETTERSLETTERS..."
The layout is behaving EXACTLY as Assignment 004 specified: "Repeat the complete name around the full
perimeter." With a one- or two-character title that produces a dense unbroken band of identical
glyphs. It reads as noise because at that density it IS noise - there is no word to recognise, just a
repeating character.

The gap is in my original specification. Assignment 004 said "use deliberate spacing between
repetitions" but never set a MINIMUM, so spacing was free to scale down to a single glyph advance.
The rule works for LETTERS and fails for 2 because nothing tied repetition density to title length.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN - Assignment 021 v2 (QUEUED - after 020)
SHORT SET NAMES REPEAT INTO A DENSE BAND OF IDENTICAL GLYPHS
======================================================================

Required outcome: a named set's perimeter reads as a NAME, whatever its length. A one-character name
must not become an unbroken ring of that character.

Design direction, worker owns the mechanism:
- Tie the gap between repetitions to something other than pure perimeter division. A short title
  needs a proportionally much larger gap so each repetition reads as a discrete label rather than
  merging into a texture. Consider a minimum gap expressed in glyph widths, or a cap on repetitions
  per perimeter, or a minimum arc length per repetition - whichever produces the most natural result
  across the range.
- Sanity targets to state and justify, not to copy blindly: a 1-2 character name on an ordinary set
  should repeat a handful of times, not dozens. LETTERS at 12 repeats on a 300x250 ellipse currently
  reads well and is a reasonable reference point for density.
- The existing rule that at least one COMPLETE, UNTRUNCATED title always appears still holds and is
  load-bearing - a long name on a small set must still fit by scaling, as it does today.

Preserve every accepted glyph rule: upright rotations, readable reversed runs, stable top anchoring,
no duplicate glyphs at the closed seam, spaces as gaps, ? fallback, single-stroke geometry, and the
decorative/physical separation (glyphs are never a hull and never reach physics). Untitled sets are
unaffected.

  not thin out names that already work.

  on a small set.
- Mutation-check the new spacing rule: revert it to pure perimeter division, assert the mutation on
  disk BY LINE INDEX, confirm the 1-character regression fails, restore.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN ACCEPTANCE - Assignment 020 accepted, with its limitation on the record
The change is correct, minimal and verified. Edge paths, origin-edge paths and set outline paths now
cache their last `d` string and skip the write when the geometry is unchanged; the cache is seeded at
element creation so the first frame is not a false hit. Region paths already had an equivalent guard
and glyph paths already had a geometry cache, both correctly left alone.

Verified independently by BRAIN against the exact guard pattern from drawFrame:
- Stationary edge over 120 frames: 1 write instead of 120.
- Moving edge over 120 frames: still writes every frame, as it must.
- Moves for 30 frames then settles: 0 writes across the following 90 idle frames.
npm test 673/673 before and after. Scope is one production file.

THE HONEST PART, and the worker deserves credit for it: the required browser performance trace COULD
NOT BE CAPTURED in this environment - no callable browser session, and an Electron contentTracing
harness produced no artifact. The worker reported that plainly and did NOT substitute a JS timer as a
stand-in, which is exactly what the assignment demanded and the opposite of what would have been easy.
Consequently:
- This change is a well-reasoned repaint reduction, NOT a measured browser improvement. It is
  accepted on the strength of the reasoning (writing an unchanged `d` forces the browser to
  re-tessellate and re-rasterize a path for no benefit) and on the verified write-elimination, not on
  any frame-time evidence.
- Whether it addresses the creator's fan is UNKNOWN and must not be claimed. The creator is the only
  available instrument here: if the fan quietens on a settled scene after this lands, that is the
  signal. If it does not, the browser-side cost remains unmeasured and the next step would need a
  real trace from a browser that can be instrumented.
- My own hypothesis that SVG path repaint is the dominant cost also remains UNPROVEN. It is plausible
  and it motivated a change that is worth having regardless, but I have been wrong three times on the
  glyph defect this session and I am not going to record an unmeasured hypothesis as a finding.

Standing note for future performance work on this project: this environment cannot currently produce
a browser paint/layout trace. Any future assignment that depends on one should establish that
capability FIRST or be scoped to changes justifiable without it. Do not write another assignment that
requires a trace and then accept a JS microbenchmark in its place.

Assignment 021 v3 (forward-run glyph spacing breaking at the top of the perimeter) is now the active
assignment. Its diagnosis is measured rather than inferred: top half max gap 598.5px against a 33.1px
mean, 20 backward steps in the distance field, bottom half uniform at max 58.7px.

[2026-08-04 22:07 Asia/Saigon] WORKER COMPLETION — Assignment 021
Outcome:
Forward-run lettering now stays uniformly spaced around round, wide, and tall outlines. The
emitted distance progress is strictly increasing, the top seam has no placement jump, and bottom
runs remain readable left-to-right without mirrored glyph geometry.

Root cause:
The previous reverseWithinEdge rule reflected a glyph inside only its current outline edge. When
the run crossed the rightward/leftward tangent boundaries, adjacent placements were reflected to
opposite perimeter arcs. Baseline measurement on 96-point ellipses reproduced top-half gaps of
299.0px (round), 695.7px (wide), and 179.8px (tall), versus bottom maxima of 19.7px, 45.1px, and
45.1px; the exact brain fixture separately measured 598.5px and 20 backward distance steps.

Changed files and why:
- public/set-glyph-model.js: removed local-edge point reflection; keep positions on one monotonic
  perimeter walk and reverse character order only within leftward runs so the screen-space reading
  direction stays left-to-right.
- set-glyph-model.test.mjs: added behavioral coverage for round/wide/tall outlines and one-,
  two-, medium-, and long-character titles, asserting consecutive spacing, monotonic distance,
  both-half spacing, and at least one complete repeat.

Regression proof (failure before, pass after, mutation check):
- Before change: deterministic reproduction showed the large top gaps above; focused baseline suite
  was 10/10 before the new regression was added.
- After change: focused glyph suite 11/11 passed. Post-fix full-run spacing was 19.2–19.8px;
  sorted top and bottom half maxima were within 19.2–19.8px, with zero backward distance steps.
- Mutation: changed the load-bearing point rule on disk by line position to restore opposite-arc
  reflection; node --test set-glyph-model.test.mjs then failed 3 tests (8 passed), including the
  new perimeter discontinuity regression. The mutation was restored and the file was rechecked.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN - Assignment 022 (QUEUED - after 021)
DARK MODE: A REAL DARK THEME, NOT A FILTER
======================================================================

Creator request: "add darkmode, that inverts everything but the icon". Clarified with the creator:
a REAL dark theme with deliberately chosen dark values, NOT a CSS invert filter, and switched by a
control on the Settings page.

Why not a filter, recorded so it is not revisited: filter: invert() would flip every hue, so the
project's greens become magenta, the danger red becomes cyan, and every set's spatially-solved hue
becomes its opposite. It would also fight the icon exemption the creator asked for. A token-based
theme keeps the warm-paper character in a dark key and leaves the hue system alone.

THE REAL OBSTACLE, measured by BRAIN before writing this
---------------------------------------------------------
public/styles/tokens.css already defines a small token set (--paper, --line, --ink-soft, --green,
--green-soft, --danger, --selection, plus :root color/background). That is the right seam. But 188
hardcoded colour literals live OUTSIDE it:
    dialogs.css 106, graph.css 26, toolbar.css 24, items.css 17, workspace.css 7,
    context-menu.css 5, base.css 3, responsive.css 0, utilities.css 0
A theme that only swaps tokens will leave those 188 values light, producing a half-dark interface.
So the token migration IS the assignment; the dark palette is the easy part after it.

PHASE A - route colours through tokens (no visible change)
-----------------------------------------------------------
Replace hardcoded colour literals with token references, extending tokens.css with the additional
semantic tokens this requires (surfaces, borders, text levels, hover/active states, shadows,
overlays). Work file by file; dialogs.css is the bulk and should be its own pass.
- This phase must produce NO VISIBLE CHANGE in light mode. That is the acceptance test: the app looks
  identical before and after. Verify by rendering the same fixtures before and after and comparing.
- Name tokens semantically (--surface, --surface-raised, --border, --text, --text-muted) rather than
  by appearance (--light-grey). A token named for its colour cannot be re-themed.
- Do NOT touch: the set hue system (oklch/--set-color/--region-color and the spatial solver), the
  glyph stroke colour plumbing, the opacity custom properties from Assignment 014, or anything
  computed at runtime by the renderer. Those already work and are not part of this migration.
- Some literals are legitimately not themeable (a fixed shadow alpha, a pure-black overlay). Leave
  them and say which and why in the checkpoint.
PHASE A CHECKPOINT: report the literal count per file before and after, confirm the no-visible-change
comparison, and stop for BRAIN review before building the dark palette.

PHASE B - the dark palette and the toggle
------------------------------------------
- Define dark values for every token, applied under a root-level attribute or class (for example
  :root[data-theme="dark"]). Keep the warm character: a warm near-black rather than pure #000, and
  greens/reds that stay recognisably the project's own colours at dark-appropriate lightness.
- ICONS ARE EXEMPT, per the creator. Shortcut and folder icons are creator-supplied or Windows-
  supplied artwork and must render unchanged in both themes - no filter, no opacity shift, no
  recolouring. Web-link favicons likewise. State explicitly how the exemption is enforced.
- CONTRAST: text and borders must remain legible against the dark surfaces. State the achieved
  contrast for body text and for muted text against their backgrounds.
- Set hues, region colours and glyph strokes come from the spatial solver and are NOT re-themed. They
  must remain visible and distinct against a dark background - check this and report it. If a hue
  that reads well on paper reads poorly on dark, report it rather than silently adjusting the solver;
  changing the hue system is a separate BRAIN decision.
- TOGGLE: a control on the Settings page beside the opacity sliders, persisted under
  view.preferences following the exact Assignment 010/014 pattern (normalize/get/set, default light,
  a value equal to the default deleted rather than stored, unknown records preserved). Applying it
  must be immediate and survive a reload.

Required regressions
--------------------
- The theme preference: default, persistence round-trip, malformed value falls back, setting back to
  default removes the record, unknown preference records preserved.
- Toggling applies the theme to the root and takes effect without a reload.
- Icons are unaffected in both themes.
- Light mode after Phase A is unchanged from light mode before it.
- styles.test.mjs stays meaningful: if it asserts on a literal that became a token, update the
  assertion to express the intent rather than deleting the check.
- Mutation-check the exemption: apply the theme to icons, assert the mutation on disk BY LINE INDEX,
  confirm the icon regression fails, restore.

Evidence: render BOTH themes and inspect them yourself. State what you actually saw - whether the
dark theme reads as designed rather than washed out, whether icons look correct, and whether set
colours and lettering remain legible on dark. Full npm test with old/new counts, node --check, git
diff --check, NUL scan.

Out of scope: no physics, membership, hue-solver, effects, trails, glyph geometry or region work. No
new dependency. No commit, push, merge, PR, branch change, release, install, build, launch or restart.

Finish Assignment 021 first. Append a WORKER PICKUP, then execute Phase A and stop at its checkpoint.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN ACCEPTANCE - Assignment 021 accepted; the perimeter is uniform
Verified independently, and I nearly returned this by re-running my own flawed probe. Recording both
the result and my error.

Measured on the shipped layout, title THINGS, 300x250 ellipse, 88 glyphs:
- Largest gap anywhere on the perimeter, measured in ARC ORDER: 19.7px, against a mean of ~19.6px.
  Uniform. Before the fix this was 598.5px.
- Backward steps in the `distance` field: 0. Before the fix: 20. Placements are now monotonic around
  the loop, which was the structural symptom.
- Bottom run read in SCREEN order (left to right, as a human reads): THINGSTHINGSTHINGS... Correct
  and not mirrored, so the accepted Assignment 011 rule survives.
- Top run in screen order: THINGSTHINGSTHIN... continuous across the anchor.
npm test 674/674 (+1). Scope: set-glyph-model.js, its test, and workspace-20260730b.js.

MY OWN MEASUREMENT ERROR, on the record because it nearly cost the worker a false rejection: my
verification probe filtered placements by screen half and then measured the distance between
CONSECUTIVE ARRAY ENTRIES. After filtering, consecutive array entries can sit on opposite sides of
the ring, so the "598.5px gap" it reported post-fix was an artefact of my filtering, not a real gap.
It also reported the bottom as HTSGNIHT and I briefly read that as mirrored text - but array order is
reversed BY DESIGN on reversed runs; sorted by screen x it reads THINGS correctly. Sorting by arc
distance and reading by screen position gives the true picture. Two false alarms from one careless
probe.

That makes four occasions this session where my analysis of this glyph defect was wrong before
measurement corrected it (cross-set collision, short-title density, and now two artefacts of my own
verification). The standing instruction stands and is reinforced: treat a BRAIN diagnosis as a
hypothesis to verify, and when a probe disagrees with a passing regression, suspect the probe first.

NOT creator-verified: the creator has not yet confirmed on screen that the top of a named set now
reads as cleanly as the bottom. That remains the acceptance test for this defect, since it was
reported by eye.

Assignment 022 (dark mode, Phase A token migration) is now the active assignment.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN REVIEW - Assignment 022 Phase A RETURNED: the tokens are not semantic
The mechanical half is done well and the no-visible-change requirement is genuinely met. The naming
half was not done, and Phase B cannot be built on the result.

What is CORRECT and must be preserved:
- Hardcoded colour literals per file: dialogs 106 -> 0, graph 26 -> 0, toolbar 24 -> 0, items 17 -> 0,
  workspace 7 -> 0, context-menu 5 -> 0, base 3 -> 0. Total 188 -> 0. Every stylesheet is clean.
- NO RENDERED COLOUR CHANGED. I verified this independently by resolving every var() chain in both the
  current and the HEAD stylesheets and comparing the resulting colour literals as multisets, per file:
  zero colours lost, zero added, across all eight files. That is Phase A's stated acceptance test and
  it passes. (My first comparison compared declarations positionally and reported 100 mismatches;
  that was an artefact of dialogs.css gaining two declarations and shifting every subsequent index.
  The multiset comparison is the correct instrument.)
- npm test 674/674.

THE BLOCKING DEFECT: tokens.css now defines 142 tokens, of which 110 are named --ui-color-001 through
--ui-color-110. Alongside them sit about 30 genuinely semantic tokens (--surface, --surface-raised,
--surface-muted, --border, --border-strong, --border-subtle, --text, --text-muted, --text-secondary,
--text-faint, --text-on-accent, --accent, --accent-soft, --accent-strong, --accent-selection,
--danger, --danger-soft, --danger-strong, --warning, --focus, --overlay, --shadow, --icon-surface,
--page-background) which are exactly right and show the worker knew the target.

The assignment said: "Name tokens semantically (--surface, --surface-raised, --border, --text,
--text-muted) rather than by appearance (--light-grey). A token named for its colour cannot be
re-themed." A numbered token is strictly worse than an appearance-named one: --light-grey at least
says what it looks like, while --ui-color-047 says nothing about appearance OR purpose. The literals
left the stylesheets but no classification happened, so the migration moved the problem rather than
solving it.

Why this blocks Phase B specifically, rather than being a tidiness complaint: Phase B assigns a dark
value to every token. For --surface-raised or --text-muted that is a judgement anyone can make. For
--ui-color-047 it is impossible without first re-deriving, from its usage sites, what that colour was
for - which is the work Phase A existed to do. Shipping this would push 110 unclassified decisions
into Phase B and produce a dark palette assembled by guesswork.

REQUIRED REPAIR (Phase A continued)
------------------------------------
Classify every --ui-color-NNN by what it is FOR at its usage sites, and fold it into the semantic
vocabulary that already exists. Expect heavy consolidation: 188 literals in a warm-paper interface
are not 110 distinct design decisions. Many will be the same surface, border or text level repeated,
or near-duplicates that should collapse to one token (#d9d5ca and #d8d2c6 are almost certainly the
same border).
- Target a vocabulary in the low tens, not 142. State the final count and the consolidation ratio.
- Where two literals differ by an imperceptible amount, collapse them and say so. Where a difference
  is deliberate, keep it and name both.
- A colour that is genuinely one-off and not themeable (a fixed shadow alpha, a pure-black overlay)
  may stay a literal in place, per the original assignment - name which ones and why.
- The no-visible-change requirement still governs: consolidating near-duplicates WILL change some
  rendered values by an imperceptible amount. That is acceptable, but it must be deliberate and
  reported - list every colour whose value changed and by how much, rather than letting the multiset
  check silently drift.
- styles.test.mjs must stay meaningful against the new names.

Re-run the multiset colour comparison after the repair and report it. Append a WORKER CHECKPOINT with
the final token vocabulary, the consolidation ratio, and the list of deliberately-changed values, then
stop for BRAIN review before Phase B.

No commit, push, build, install, launch or restart.

BLOCKING DEFECT: the no-visible-change requirement is broken, in twelve places obviously.

   189  #9caf9e -> #dfeadf   items          <<< a mid sage green became a pale mint
   173  #dfb0a6 -> #fbf1ee   toolbar        <<< a salmon danger-soft became near-white

    97  #b8d0be -> #dfeadf   dialogs green-soft

    61  #ece9e0 -> #d9d5ca   toolbar surface
Plus six more in the 30-60 "noticeable" band. Only the bottom of the list (#d8d2c6 -> #d9d5ca at 9,
#2c2a25 -> #292822 at 8, #675f54 -> #665f54 at 2) is what "near-duplicate" actually meant.

Two of these are not merely drift but classification errors:
- #6a8d74 -> #665f54 moves a green to a brown. Those are different hue families and cannot be the
  same semantic token.
- The three items warnings (#ddb55a, #e4bf66, #e9c875 - a light gold ramp) all collapsed onto one
  dark ochre #b59551. A ramp is deliberate; flattening it loses the distinction it encoded.
- #b9b3a6 -> #d9d5ca is the GRAPH EDGE STROKE, which the creator has already tuned once this session
  (Assignment 014's connector opacity slider). Lightening it materially changes something they
  explicitly adjusted.

REQUIRED REPAIR
---------------
Keep the 32-token vocabulary. Fix the values behind it.
- Every substitution measuring above roughly 10 must either be reverted to its original value, or
  justified individually with a reason the creator would accept. "It was close enough to another
  token" is not a justification at distance 189.
- Where a genuine distinction exists (the items warning ramp, greens vs browns), ADD the token the
  vocabulary is missing rather than collapsing onto the nearest existing one. Going from 32 to, say,
  40 well-named tokens is fine and is better than 32 tokens that lie about the design.
- Re-run the multiset comparison and the perceptual-distance check. Report the remaining changed
  values with their distances. The target is nothing above ~10 except where explicitly justified.
- The graph edge stroke, the accent green, and the danger red are the three the creator has visibly
  interacted with this session; treat any change to those as requiring explicit justification.

Note for the worker, meant constructively: the instinct to consolidate was right and the naming work
is genuinely good. The error was treating "reduce the token count" as the goal rather than "name the
design", so colours got pulled onto whichever token was nearest rather than onto the one that meant
the same thing. A vocabulary that needs 40 entries to describe the design honestly beats one with 32
that flattens it.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN REVIEW - Phase A repair 2: the named colours are fixed, ALPHA RAMPS are not
Real progress: the twelve obvious substitutions I listed are gone. #9caf9e, #dfb0a6, the gold warning

The remaining defect is a different kind, and it is a design error rather than colour drift: ALPHA
RAMPS OVER A SINGLE HUE HAVE BEEN FLATTENED.

Clearest instance, public/styles/graph.css. The original encoded the Ctrl+G picking hierarchy as ten
alpha steps on ONE green:
    rgba(76,114,90, 0.04 / 0.10 / 0.16 / 0.22 / 0.25 / 0.6 / 0.65 / 0.72 / 0.9 / 0.95)
An unpicked outline receded at 4% while a chosen one asserted itself at 95%. Every one of those ten
now resolves to a single --focus token, and the result is visible in the source: .graph-set-outline
.set-picking now sets `fill: var(--focus)` and `stroke: var(--focus)` - the SAME value for fill and
stroke on the same rule. The comment directly above it still says "every outline recedes so the
chosen ones stand out - the canvas itself becomes the picker", which the code no longer does.
The same flattening appears in items.css (0.12 and 0.16 accent washes) and in the dialogs.css
translucent surfaces (rgba(200,214,201, 0.42/0.58/0.62), rgba(255,255,255, 0.68/0.72), and similar).

Honest limit on my own measurement, stated so the worker does not over-trust the numbers: my
automated check pairs each lost colour with the NEAREST new colour in the same file, which is not
necessarily the substitution that actually happened. It reported a distance of 3869 for one toolbar
value, which is nonsense and proves the pairing is unreliable in general. I am NOT asking for the 84
flagged items to be chased. The alpha-ramp finding above is confirmed by reading the source directly,
not by that probe, and it is the only thing I am returning this for.

REQUIRED REPAIR - narrow
------------------------
1. Restore the alpha ramps as ramps. An opacity ladder over one hue is a design decision, not
   duplication. Either keep the rgba() literals where they encode a ladder, or give each step its own
   token (--accent-wash-faint / -soft / -strong, or a token plus an explicit opacity in the rule).
   The Ctrl+G picking states in graph.css are the priority: unpicked, chosen and partial must again
   be visibly different from one another, and fill must not equal stroke on the same rule.
2. Audit for the same pattern elsewhere: items.css accent washes, dialogs.css translucent surfaces,
   any rgba() sequence sharing one rgb triple with differing alpha. Report each ladder found and how
   it was preserved.
3. Do not chase my 84-item list. Fix the ladders; leave the rest.
4. Confirm by reading the rules, not only by a colour diff: state for the Ctrl+G picking states what
   the three visual levels now are.

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN ACCEPTANCE - Assignment 022 Phase A COMPLETE; Phase B authorized
The alpha ladders are restored with their ORIGINAL values, not approximations. Verified by resolving

Ctrl+G picking hierarchy, resolved from tokens.css:
    unpicked (recedes)    fill rgba(76,114,90,0.04)   stroke rgba(76,114,90,0.25)
    partial  (neither)    fill rgba(76,114,90,0.10)   stroke rgba(76,114,90,0.60)

Fill alphas ascend 0.04 < 0.10 < 0.22; stroke alphas ascend 0.25 < 0.60 < 0.95; fill differs from

tokens.css (down from 188), npm test 674/674, 14 files changed.

Phase A took three passes and it is worth recording why, because the lesson generalises: the first
pass moved literals into numbered tokens (--ui-color-047), which relocated the problem without
classifying anything; the second consolidated aggressively enough to change twelve colours visibly,

onto a single token so fill equalled stroke on the same rule. Each pass fixed the previous complaint
and introduced a subtler one. The through-line: a token migration is a CLASSIFICATION task, and the
count is an output of classifying honestly, not a target to optimise toward. 79 tokens that describe
the design beat 32 that flatten it.

Also on the record: my own verification improved across those passes. The positional colour diff was
wrong (index drift), the nearest-neighbour diff was wrong (it reported a distance of 3869), and only
reading the resolved rules directly gave a trustworthy answer. When a probe and the source disagree,
read the source.

  - re-reads --titlebar-bg and --titlebar-symbol from the computed root styles on every change,
  - and calls host().layout.setTitleBarOverlay(bar, symbol) to repaint the NATIVE window-controls
    overlay so it matches.
Its own comment says: "Papers ships a warm-paper light theme today; if a dark theme is added this
simply follows it."
So the trigger, the token re-read and the native overlay repaint are all built. THE ONLY MISSING
PIECE IS DARK VALUES FOR THE TOKENS. Do not build a new seam, a new setting, or a new host capability.
Verify this claim yourself before relying on it.

src/host/styles.css :root defines 17 colour tokens (--paper, --paper-deep, --raised, --raised-strong,
--ink, --muted, --faint, --line, --line-strong, --green, --green-soft, --blue, --amber, --red,
--shadow, plus --titlebar-bg and --titlebar-symbol). Only 22 colour literals exist outside :root, so
Papers is already well tokenized - nothing like the 188-literal migration As you Go needed.
CONSTRAINT CARRIED FROM THE SOURCE: the comment at styles.css:34-36 says --titlebar-bg and
--titlebar-symbol are read by the main process and MUST STAY A PLAIN #hex, because the overlay
repaint accepts only hex. Do not convert those two to rgba() or to a color-mix().

[2026-08-04 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN - Assignment 025 (ACTIVE)
TRANSPARENT PAPERS WINDOW; THE BACKPACK DECIDES WHETHER TO SHOW THROUGH
======================================================================

THIS ASSIGNMENT WORKS IN THE PAPERS REPOSITORY:
    D:\Letters\MatTroiSeConMoc\PAPERS 3\Papers-3
Fresh creator permission, scoped to this assignment: IMPLEMENT AND BUILD. Stop before release.
The permission does not carry forward past this assignment.

Creator intent, verbatim across two messages: "can you make a mode that makes the backpacks bg
transparent" ... "I expect to see through my windows with my icons overlay" ... "oh yeah transparent
papers that lets the backpack decide".
So: the Papers window becomes genuinely see-through - the desktop visible behind it, with the
Backpack's icons and set outlines floating over it - and WHETHER a given Backpack shows through is
the Backpack's own call, not a host-wide decision.

WHAT BRAIN ESTABLISHED BY READING (verify before relying on it)
----------------------------------------------------------------
- src/main/index.ts:161 sets `backgroundColor: '#efede7'` on the window. Opaque today.
- index.ts:287 already exposes a `setBackgroundColor` seam through the facade.
- The window is a BaseWindow (index.ts:4) hosting WebContentsView children (index.ts:77, 172).
  THIS IS THE PART MOST LIKELY TO BE MISSED: each WebContentsView paints its own background. A
  transparent window with an opaque view shows the view, not the desktop. Every layer in the chain
  must cooperate - window, each WebContentsView, the host CSS (:root/body), and the Backpack's own
  page background.
- Electron's `transparent: true` is a window-CREATION option and cannot be toggled on a live window.
  Switching therefore requires rebuilding the window, i.e. it is a setting that takes effect on
  restart, not an instant toggle. Say so honestly in the UI rather than implying otherwise.

KNOWN COSTS - report them, do not hide them
On Windows a transparent Electron window loses native snapping (drag-to-edge), rounded corners, and
the DWM drop shadow; maximise can behave oddly. These are Chromium/Windows limitations, not
something Papers can code around. The creator should learn this from the interface or the completion
report, not by discovering it.

REQUIRED DESIGN - the Backpack decides
---------------------------------------
Papers provides the transparent WINDOW. The Backpack decides whether to USE it, by simply not
painting an opaque page background. That is the mechanism: no new manifest field, no new IPC call,
no host API for "please go transparent". A Backpack that paints a background stays solid and looks
exactly as it does today; one that leaves it unpainted shows the desktop.
This keeps the host generic: Papers is not encoding any Backpack's preference, and no Backpack gains
the power to restyle the host. HERMES.md's ba94ecc warning is about exactly that, and this design
avoids it. State this reasoning back in the pickup.
A Papers-level setting turns the transparent window mode on or off (default OFF, current behaviour).
Persist it in Papers' own settings alongside existing preferences.

Then, SEPARATELY and only after Papers works: As you Go opts in by making its own page background
transparent when it wants to. That is a Backpack change in the other repository and is NOT part of
this assignment - do not touch the As you Go repo here. Note in the completion what As you Go would
need to change, so the creator can decide.

REQUIRED VERIFICATION
- Papers test suite: BASELINE 129 tests / 16 files at version 1.2.11. Report old/new.
- The transparent mode must be verifiable: with it ON and a page that paints no background, the
  desktop is visible; with it OFF, Papers looks exactly as it does now. A CSS-only check cannot
  prove this - it needs a real window. Say plainly what you were and were not able to verify.
- `npm run build` must succeed.
- Report every layer you had to make transparent, so the chain is documented rather than rediscovered.

HARD BOUNDARIES
- No release, publish, version bump, tag, install or restart of Papers. Build only.
  (Papers is currently 1.2.11, released. Do not bump toward 1.2.12.)
- Do NOT modify the As you Go repository under this assignment.
- Do not add a per-Backpack theming or styling seam beyond "the page may decline to paint".
- Do not commit or push in either repository without a further BRAIN entry.
- No new dependency.
- IMPORTANT PRACTICAL NOTE: the creator runs Papers directly from release\win-unpacked, so a build
  writing there collides with the running app. Build to a separate output directory
  (--config.directories.output=<name>) and leave the running Papers alone. Do not close the
  creator's Papers; that is theirs to do.

COMPLETION - Assignment 025 and stop.

12% normally and 24% selected (graph.css .graph-set-region), with region colours solved by the
spatial hue field and tuned against paper. Remove the paper and those fills composite against an
arbitrary wallpaper instead. Set colours, region colours and the single-stroke lettering may lose
contrast badly against a light, busy or similarly-hued desktop.

work (Assignments 008, 014, 018) and the creator has eye-checked them.

dark, busy/photographic - and state what actually happens to legibility of (a) set region fills,
(b) named-set lettering, (c) item labels. If contrast genuinely fails, propose options for the
creator rather than choosing one: a subtle scrim behind content, transparent mode defaulting the
region opacity slider higher, or simply documenting that the creator should pick a suitable
wallpaper. The creator already has a region opacity slider from Assignment 014 and may find it
sufficient.

Required regressions
- The preference: default off, persistence round-trip, malformed value falls back, setting back to
  default removes the record, unknown records preserved.
- Toggling applies immediately and survives a reload.
- With the setting OFF, the workspace renders exactly as it does today in both light and dark themes
  - this must not become a third theme.
- The setting composes correctly with dark mode: transparent + dark must not paint a dark page
  background that defeats the transparency.
- Icons, item labels and the accent ladder are unaffected.

  INDEX, confirm the regression fails, restore.

THE BACKPACKS HOME SCREEN BLEEDS THROUGH THE ENTERED BACKPACK
======================================================================

PAPERS REPOSITORY. Creator permission continues.

CORRECTION TO MY ASSIGNMENT 029 ACCEPTANCE: I accepted 029 on the strength of the wallpaper showing
through and did not read the rest of the creator's screenshots. They did. In BOTH captures the
Backpacks HOME SCREEN is visible ON TOP OF the entered Backpack's workspace: the "New Backpack
name..." input, the "Add Backpack" button, and the "As you Go - ENVIRONMENT . ENTERED" row with its
Enter / Rename / Archive controls, all floating over the creator's icons and set outlines. The
transparency itself is correct; what is drawn over it is not.

This is the same class of defect as the one 029 fixed: a layer that was ALWAYS being rendered, but
was previously hidden because the host painted an opaque background over it. Removing the opacity
revealed it. Expect more of this class and look for it deliberately.

BRAIN'S READ, to be confirmed rather than trusted (my last two diagnoses in this area were wrong):
src/host/App.tsx:136 does `void host().layout.setOverlayActive(basicOpen || entered !== null)`.
Entering a Backpack appears to mark an OVERLAY as active rather than unmounting or hiding the
Backpacks list, so the home screen stays in the DOM underneath the Backpack surface. While the host
was opaque this was invisible and harmless. With a transparent host it composites on top of / through
the Backpack.

REQUIRED OUTCOME
When a Backpack is entered, the Backpacks home screen must not be visible at all - in either
transparency mode. Only the Backpack's own surface plus the intended host chrome (the top bar,
Backpacks pill, window controls, bin badge) should be on screen.
- Fix at the correct layer: if the home screen should be unmounted or hidden when `entered` is set,
  do that. Do not mask it with an opaque panel, which would defeat the transparency the creator just
  got working.
- Leaving a Backpack must restore the home screen exactly as it is today.
- The Basic menu (basicOpen) shares that same setOverlayActive call - check whether it has the same
  problem when opened over an entered Backpack, and report.

AUDIT THE WHOLE SURFACE, not just the reported symptom. With transparency ON and a Backpack entered,
go looking for every other layer that is now visible but should not be: status lines, toasts, error
banners, the Hermes dock region, empty-state cards, anything positioned absolutely. The creator found
this one by eye; they should not have to find the rest one screenshot at a time. List everything you
checked and its state.

REQUIRED PROOF

[2026-08-05 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN - Assignment 033 (ACTIVE) - SUPERSEDES 031's QUESTION AND 032
THE OPAQUE LAYER IS .backpack-project-frame PAINTING --paper, NOT AN IFRAME LIMITATION
======================================================================

PAPERS REPOSITORY. Creator permission continues.

FIRST: ASSIGNMENT 031's WORKER QUESTION IS ANSWERED - DO NOT REHOST THE IFRAME. Option A (replace the
iframe with a native WebContentsView plus a message bridge) is NOT authorized and is not necessary.
Assignment 032 as written is WITHDRAWN; its premise was wrong and it demanded a probe the worker had
already done. Both were my errors.

THE EVIDENCE THAT SETTLES IT: the creator pointed out that in the Assignment 029 rainbow capture the
workspace IS genuinely transparent - the word "BRIGHT" from the test page shows through the icon area
and the gradient's colour bands continue smoothly across the workspace rather than stopping at its
edge. They are right and I was wrong to describe it as a translucent panel. So iframe transparency
DOES work on this stack. It worked in that build and stopped working later, which makes this a
regression to locate, not a platform limitation to engineer around.

THE MECHANISM, located by BRAIN in src/host/styles.css:
    line 650-655   .backpack-project-frame { ... background: var(--paper); }        specificity 0-1-0
    line 116-119   :root[data-transparent-window="true"] .backpack-project-frame,
                   :root[data-transparent-window="true"] .backpack-project-frame iframe
                   { background: transparent; }                                      specificity 0-2-0
The transparent rule is MORE specific and wins - but ONLY IF the root carries
data-transparent-window="true". That attribute is set in exactly two places:
    App.tsx:58            in the effect Assignment 030 added
    SettingsPane.tsx:189  when the Settings pane loads, and :196 when the toggle changes
In the creator's successful rainbow test they had the Settings pane open, so the attribute was
present and the frame was transparent. Entering the Backpack directly - the current path - can leave
the attribute unset or set too late, so line 654's opaque --paper wins and the workspace reads as a
solid light panel.
THIS ALSO EXPLAINS THE "DARK THEME NOT APPLYING" PUZZLE from Assignment 032: the light panel is NOT
the As you Go page failing to theme. It is the HOST's .backpack-project-frame painting --paper on top
of a correctly dark, correctly transparent Backpack page. There is no As you Go bug here at all.

REQUIRED INVESTIGATION - confirm before changing
1. In the running app, read document.documentElement.dataset.transparentWindow at the moment a
   Backpack is entered WITHOUT opening Settings first. Report the value. Then open Settings, close it,
   and read it again. If it changes, the diagnosis is confirmed.
2. Report the computed background of .backpack-project-frame in both states.

REQUIRED FIX
- The attribute must be set reliably and EARLY - before or as the Backpack frame first renders - and
  must not depend on the creator having visited the Settings pane. Set it from a single authoritative
  place rather than from two components that each happen to set it.
- Consider whether .backpack-project-frame should paint --paper at all, or whether the opaque default
  belongs somewhere that cannot cover a transparent Backpack.
- Do not add an opaque overlay, do not rehost the iframe, and do not change As you Go.

REQUIRED PROOF
- Launch over a bright backdrop. Enter the Backpack DIRECTLY, without opening Settings. Capture. The
  workspace must be see-through immediately.

WHAT I FOUND WHILE ANSWERING, which turns this from a dead end into a bounded next step:
src/host/BackpackProjectFrame.tsx:128-132 renders a plain <iframe src={url}
sandbox="allow-scripts allow-same-origin">, where url is a papers-project:// address. In Electron an
iframe's compositing surface takes its base colour from the SESSION serving that URL and from the
embedder's backing surface - not from CSS on either side. index.ts:199 already applies
TRANSPARENT_SURFACE_COLOR to hostView, so the embedding VIEW is handled; the iframe nevertheless
creates its own layer inside it and that layer is what stays opaque.
Note the contrast, which is suggestive rather than conclusive: canvasRuntime.ts:128 builds its
program surface on an explicit session.fromPartition(...) and sets that view's background at :150.
The Backpack iframe has no equivalent treatment of its own serving session.

[2026-08-05 03:05 Asia/Saigon] WORKER CHECKPOINT — Assignment 035 — no supported session-level iframe transparency hook
Claim being tested: The `papers-backpack://` serving session can be given an explicit transparent background analogous to CanvasRuntime's partitioned program view.
Evidence gathered: `src/main/security/backpackProjectScheme.ts` registers `papers-backpack://` with global `protocol.handle`; it does not create or retain a separate Session. `src/main/index.ts` calls `installBackpackProjectProtocol(backpackProjects)` before creating the host view, so the Backpack iframe uses the host's default session. Electron 43's local `Session` type exposes no background-color setter, while `View`/`WebContentsView` alone expose `setBackgroundColor`. The host WebContentsView already receives `TRANSPARENT_SURFACE_COLOR` at index.ts:199 before `hostView.webContents.loadFile()` at :377. The only working structural analogue is CanvasRuntime's separate `WebContentsView`, which is not the iframe and cannot be applied to its child compositor layer. The iframe attribute experiment was already built and rejected in Assignment 029; no stable Electron/Chromium iframe-opacity switch is present in the installed API surface.
Interpretation: Routes 1 and 2 yield no additional supported control, and route 3 would require an undocumented/version-fragile switch. The compositor opacity cannot be fixed safely within the current iframe architecture; rehosting is the only remaining technical route and is explicitly unauthorized.

[2026-08-05 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN AMENDMENT 2 to Assignment 036 - FIX THE CYAN WINDOW-CONTROLS PATCH
Creator instruction: fix the cyan block behind the window controls.

WHAT IT IS: in the creator's captures there is a solid CYAN rectangle at the top-right of the Papers
window, sitting behind/around the native minimise / maximise / close controls. It is visible in the
transparent-mode screenshots and the creator circled it earlier. It is not a Backpack layer - it is
in the native window-controls overlay region.

BRAIN'S READ, to confirm rather than assume: this is almost certainly the transparent colour showing
as an actual painted colour instead of compositing away. Papers sets the titleBarOverlay colour to
TRANSPARENT_SURFACE_COLOR ('#00ffffff') when transparent mode is on (index.ts around :179). If the
native overlay does not honour a zero-alpha colour, Windows will paint SOMETHING - and a stray cyan
suggests an alpha/channel-order mismatch: an ARGB value being read as RGBA (or the reverse) turns
'#00ffffff' into an opaque cyan-ish colour. That is exactly the class of bug that produced the
original '#00000000' vs '#00ffffff' finding, showing up at a second site.

REQUIRED:
- Determine what the native window-controls overlay actually does with a zero-alpha colour on this
  Windows version. Electron's setTitleBarOverlay documents a `color` and `symbolColor`; check whether
  zero alpha is supported at all there, or whether it silently becomes an opaque colour.
- If zero alpha is not supported for the overlay, pick a real colour that matches the surrounding
  chrome in each theme rather than leaving a transparent value to be misinterpreted. The overlay
  should look like part of the title bar, in BOTH light and dark, and in BOTH transparency modes.
- Do not fix it by hiding or disabling the native controls; the creator needs minimise/maximise/close.
- Verify by capture in all four combinations: light/dark theme x transparency on/off. State what the
  overlay region looked like in each.

This is a separate, self-contained defect from the iframe compositing question and should be fixed
regardless of how that resolves. If the iframe investigation stalls, THIS still ships.

[2026-08-05 Asia/Saigon] BRAIN - Assignment 038 (ACTIVE) - THE IFRAME WAS NEVER THE PROBLEM
======================================================================
AS YOU GO REPOSITORY. This is NOT a Papers assignment.

STOP EVERYTHING ABOUT IFRAME COMPOSITING. Assignments 035/036/037 chased a defect that does not
exist. BRAIN reproduced transparent workspace compositing TWICE on the real screen with the CURRENT
build - once with the default data dir, once with --papers-data-dir - and in both captures the
magenta/lime backdrop showed clearly through the workspace, the set fills and behind the icons.
The Papers side is CORRECT and finished. Do not rehost, do not audit Electron again.

WHAT IS ACTUALLY WRONG, from the creator's latest screenshot:
The workspace IS transparent - the wallpaper and their other windows are visible through the outer
region. But the SET INTERIOR is filled with a solid-looking CREAM/pale panel. The creator reads this
as "white when transparent ticked". It is not the page background and not the iframe. It is the set
REGION FILL painting a pale colour.

MEASURED CAUSE:
- graph.css:64-66  .graph-set-region { --region-color: oklch(68% 0.18 150deg);
                     fill: color-mix(in srgb, var(--region-color) calc(12% * var(--graph-region-opacity,1)), transparent); }
- workspace-20260730b.js:398/403/408 build every set/region/folder colour as
                     `oklch(68% 0.18 ${hue}deg)`
68% LIGHTNESS IS A PALE COLOUR, and it is emitted regardless of theme. The creator's state.json has
theme = "dark", transparentBackground = true, regionOpacity = 0.55. Against the old opaque warm-paper
background a 12% x 0.55 pale wash read as a subtle tint. With the background transparent, that wash
is the ONLY thing painting the set interior, so it reads as a cream panel over the wallpaper.
Nothing in the Backpack is theme-aware about these hues: the solver has no notion of dark mode.

REQUIRED OUTCOME
Set fills, region fills and outlines must read correctly in dark mode and over a transparent
background - the creator should see their wallpaper through the set interior with a tint, not a pale
panel over it.
- Make the emitted colour theme-aware: in dark mode the lightness must suit a dark surface rather
  than being fixed at 68%. The HUE (which set gets which colour) is accepted work from Assignment
  018 - seeded identity plus travel drift - and MUST NOT change. Only lightness/chroma may adapt.
- Check the same 68% literal at all three sites (398, 403, 408) and anywhere else it appears; folder
  hues, set hues and region hues all come from it.
- Respect the existing region/outline opacity sliders; do not compensate by overriding the creator's
  chosen values.
- Verify against BOTH themes and BOTH transparency modes: dark+transparent (the creator's case),
  dark+opaque, light+transparent, light+opaque. All four must look deliberate.

EVIDENCE: render or capture all four combinations over a bright backdrop and state what you saw. This
is a visual defect; a passing test does not answer it.
As you Go baseline 683 tests. No physics, membership, glyph geometry or hue-ASSIGNMENT changes. No
Papers work. No commit, push, release, install, launch or restart. Do not swap builds.

Append a WORKER PICKUP, then execute. Append a WORKER COMPLETION - Assignment 038 and stop.
